## GHSA-v8fg-2rw7-q452 · sequelize

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 9.8 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→SQL`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from insufficient validation of user-supplied string values intended for Oracle's TO_TIMESTAMP_TZ and TO_DATE functions. When a string started with these function names, Sequelize would return the value directly without proper escaping or validation, allowing an attacker to inject arbitrary SQL after the function call.

### Impact

An attacker could inject malicious SQL queries, potentially leading to unauthorized data access, modification, or deletion, and in some cases, remote code execution depending on the database configuration and privileges.

### Key diff

```
-    if (val.startsWith('TO_TIMESTAMP') || val.startsWith('TO_DATE')) {
+    if (val.startsWith('TO_TIMESTAMP_TZ') || val.startsWith('TO_DATE')) {
+      const splitVal = val.split(/\(|\)/);
+      if (splitVal.length !== 3 || splitVal[2] !== '') {
+        throw new Error('Invalid SQL function call.');
+      }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces strict validation for strings starting with 'TO_TIMESTAMP_TZ' or 'TO_DATE' when the dialect is Oracle. It now parses the function name, parameters, and format string, ensuring they conform to expected patterns and formats using Moment.js for date validation. Any deviation from the expected structure or format now results in an error.

## GHSA-3f7w-8rr8-f37f · GitPython

2026-08-03 · Python · **HIGH** 8.1 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The GitPython library allowed arbitrary options to be passed to underlying `git tag` and `git checkout-index` commands without proper sanitization. Specifically, options like `--file` (for `git tag`) and `--prefix` (for `git checkout-index`) could be exploited by an attacker to read or write arbitrary files on the system.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve arbitrary file overwrite or arbitrary file read on the system where the GitPython application is running, potentially leading to remote code execution or sensitive data exfiltration.

### Key diff

```
--- a/git/refs/tag.py
+++ b/git/refs/tag.py
@@ -121,12 +125,21 @@ def create(
         :param kwargs:
             Additional keyword arguments to be passed to :manpage:`git-tag(1)`.
 
         :return:
             A new :class:`TagReference`.
         """
+        if not allow_unsafe_options:
+            Git.check_unsafe_options(
+                options=Git._option_candidates([], kwargs),
+                unsafe_options=cls.unsafe_git_tag_options,
+            )
+
         if "ref" in kwargs and kwargs["ref"]:
             reference = kwargs["ref"]
 
---
--- a/git/index/base.py
+++ b/git/index/base.py
@@ -1261,6 +1267,12 @@ def checkout(
             i.e. if you want :manpage:`git-checkout(1)`-like behaviour, use
             ``head.checkout`` instead of ``index.checkout``.
         """
+        if not allow_unsafe_options:
+            Git.check_unsafe_options(
+                options=Git._option_candidates([], kwargs),
+                unsafe_options=self.unsafe_git_checkout_index_options,
+            )
+
         args = ["--index"]
         if force:
             args.append("--force")
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a mechanism to explicitly disallow 'unsafe' options (like `--file`, `-F` for `git tag` and `--prefix` for `git checkout-index`) by default. A new `allow_unsafe_options` parameter is added to `create()` and `checkout()` methods, which must be explicitly set to `True` to permit these options, thus requiring developers to opt-in to potentially dangerous behavior.

## GHSA-2m8v-j782-fhvr · socket.io-parser

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from the Socket.IO parser's handling of binary attachments. Specifically, it would prematurely emit a 'decoded' event for packets that declared zero attachments, even if they were marked as binary. This allowed an attacker to send a large number of such 'zero-attachment binary' packets, which would be processed and stored in memory without ever being fully reconstructed or cleared, leading to memory exhaustion.

### Impact

An attacker could send specially crafted packets to a Socket.IO server, causing its memory to be exhausted and ultimately leading to a denial of service for legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/socket.io-parser/lib/index.ts
+++ b/packages/socket.io-parser/lib/index.ts
@@ -190,11 +190,6 @@ export class Decoder extends Emitter<{}, {}, DecoderReservedEvents> {
         packet.type = isBinaryEvent ? PacketType.EVENT : PacketType.ACK;
         // binary packet's json
         this.reconstructor = new BinaryReconstructor(packet);
-
-        // no attachments, labeled binary but no binary data to follow
-        if (packet.attachments === 0) {
-          super.emitReserved("decoded", packet);
-        }
       } else {
         // non-binary full packet
         super.emitReserved("decoded", packet);
@@ -245,7 +240,7 @@ export class Decoder extends Emitter<{}, {}, DecoderReservedEvents> {
         throw new Error("Illegal attachments");
       }
       const n = Number(buf);
-      if (!isInteger(n) || n < 0) {
+      if (!isInteger(n) || n < 1) {
         throw new Error("Illegal attachments");
       } else if (n > this.opts.maxAttachments) {
         throw new Error("too many attachments");
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the special handling for `packet.attachments === 0` in binary packets, ensuring that all binary packets, regardless of attachment count, go through the `BinaryReconstructor` flow. Additionally, it changes the validation for attachment count from `n < 0` to `n < 1`, preventing negative or zero attachment counts from being considered valid for binary packets that expect attachments.

## GHSA-7p8r-x3mc-p8w7 · fast-uri

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The fast-uri library's URI parser did not correctly handle backslashes or whitespace characters in the authority introducer (e.g., `//` in `http://host`). Unlike Node.js's URL parser, which normalizes these characters to forward slashes or strips whitespace, fast-uri would incorrectly parse such URIs, leading to a different host being identified by fast-uri compared to Node.js.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious URI that, when processed by fast-uri and then potentially by Node.js's native URL parser, would lead to host confusion. This could bypass security checks, facilitate Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) to internal resources, or enable open redirect vulnerabilities.

### Key diff

```
--- a/index.js
+++ b/index.js
@@ -265,6 +279,28 @@ function parseWithStatus (uri, opts) {
   const introducerMatch = uri.match(AUTHORITY_INTRODUCER_REGION)
   if (introducerMatch !== null) {
     const region = introducerMatch[1]
-    const normalizedRegion = region.replace(/[\t\n\r]/g, '')
+    const normalizedRegion = region.replace(/[\t\n\r]/g, '')
     // Two or more leading separators introduce an authority.
     if (normalizedRegion.length >= 2) {
       if (normalizedRegion.slice(0, 2) !== '//') {
         parsed.error = parsed.error || 'URI authority must not contain a literal backslash.'
         malformedAuthorityOrPort = true
       } else if (region.length !== normalizedRegion.length) {
         parsed.error = parsed.error || 'URI authority introducer must not contain whitespace.'
         malformedAuthorityOrPort = true
       }
     }
   }
 
   const matches = uri.match(URI_PARSE)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new regular expression, `AUTHORITY_INTRODUCER_REGION`, to specifically check the authority introducer for malformed characters like backslashes or whitespace. If such characters are found, an error is thrown, preventing the URI from being parsed incorrectly and ensuring consistency with how Node.js handles these URIs.

## GHSA-rgw5-rvv9-x895 · brace-expansion

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The previous mitigation for CVE-2026-14257 only capped the *number* of expansions, but not their total length. An attacker could craft an input that generates a large number of long strings, leading to unbounded growth of intermediate arrays and exhausting memory.

### Impact

An attacker could provide a specially crafted input string that causes the application to consume excessive memory, leading to a crash and denial of service for the application.

### Key diff

```
--- a/index.js
+++ b/index.js
@@ -6,6 +6,24 @@ const escClose = '\0CLOSE' + Math.random() + '\0'
 const escComma = '\0COMMA' + Math.random() + '\0'
 const escPeriod = '\0PERIOD' + Math.random() + '\0'
 
+// The modern release lines cap this at 100_000, but the 3.x line never
+// capped the number of expansions, so it defaults to Infinity to preserve
+// published behavior. Pass the `max` option to opt in to a cap.
+export const EXPANSION_MAX = Infinity
+
+// `EXPANSION_MAX` caps the *number* of expansions, but not their length. An
+// input like `'{a,b}'.repeat(1500)` stays under any such cap - and expands
+// freely under this line's Infinity default - while making every result
+// ~1500 characters long. The result set, and the intermediate arrays built
+// while combining brace sets, then grow large enough to exhaust memory and
+// crash the process (CVE-2026-14257). `EXPANSION_MAX_LENGTH` bounds the
+// total number of characters the accumulator may hold at any point, so
+// memory stays flat no matter how many brace groups are chained. The limit
+// sits well above any realistic expansion (100k results hitting the modern
+// lines' `EXPANSION_MAX` measure ~1M characters) so legitimate input is
+// unaffected.
+export const EXPANSION_MAX_LENGTH = 4_000_000
+
 /**
  * @return {number}
  */
@@ -68,10 +86,13 @@ function parseCommaParts (str) {
 
 /**
  * @param {string} str
+ * @param {{max?: number, maxLength?: number}} [options]
  */
-export default function expandTop (str) {
+export default function expandTop (str, options = {}) {
   if (!str) { return [] }
 
+  const { max = EXPANSION_MAX, maxLength = EXPANSION_MAX_LENGTH } = options
+
   // I don't know why Bash 4.3 does this, but it does.
   // Anything starting with {} will have the first two bytes preserved
   // but *only* at the top level, so {},a}b will not expand to anything,
@@ -82,7 +103,7 @@ export default function expandTop (str) {
     str = '\{\}' + str.slice(2)
   }
 
-  return expand(escapeBraces(str), true).map(unescapeBraces)
+  return expand(escapeBraces(str), max, maxLength, true).map(unescapeBraces)
 }
 
 /**
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `EXPANSION_MAX_LENGTH` constant and an optional `maxLength` parameter to the `expandTop` function. This new limit bounds the total number of characters the accumulator may hold at any point during expansion, preventing memory exhaustion from overly long intermediate arrays.

## GHSA-4cwx-7wf7-3272 · undici

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→HEADER`

### Root cause

The `undici` library's cache control header parser did not correctly handle degenerate `private` cache directives. Specifically, it failed to normalize field names within these directives, leading to potential issues when processing malformed headers.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious `Cache-Control` header to cause a parse-time crash (Denial of Service) or potentially trigger cross-user information disclosure by manipulating how cache directives are interpreted, leading to unintended caching behavior.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/util/cache.js
+++ b/lib/util/cache.js
@@ -270,25 +289,11 @@ function parseCacheControlHeader (header) {
                 headers[headers.length - 1] = lastHeader
               }
 
-              for (let j = 0; j < headers.length; j++) {
-                headers[j] = headers[j].trim()
-              }
-
-              if (key in output) {
-                output[key] = output[key].concat(headers)
-              } else {
-                output[key] = headers
-              }
+              addFieldNameCacheControlDirective(output, key, headers)
             }
           } else {
-            // Something like `no-cache="some-header"`
-            const fieldName = value.trim()
-
-            if (key in output) {
-              output[key] = output[key].concat(fieldName)
-            } else {
-              output[key] = [fieldName]
-            }
+            // Something like `no-cache=some-header`
+            addFieldNameCacheControlDirective(output, key, [value])
           }
 
           break
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new helper function `addFieldNameCacheControlDirective` to properly normalize and handle field names within cache control directives. This function ensures that empty or malformed field names are correctly processed, preventing crashes and ensuring consistent cache behavior.

## GHSA-539m-9xh6-q6rr · GitPython

2026-08-03 · Python · **MODERATE** 6.5 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The `unsafe_git_archive_options` denylist in GitPython was incomplete, failing to include `--add-file` and `--add-virtual-file`. This omission allowed an attacker to pass these options to `git archive` commands executed via `Repo.archive()`, bypassing intended security restrictions.

### Impact

An attacker could leverage this vulnerability to read arbitrary files from the system where the GitPython application is running, potentially leading to sensitive data exposure.

### Key diff

```
--- a/git/repo/base.py
+++ b/git/repo/base.py
@@ -172,6 +174,10 @@ class Repo:
         # Writes output to a caller-controlled filesystem path.
         "--output",
         "-o",
+        # Reads from a caller-controlled filesystem path:
+        "--add-file",
+        # Injects a caller-controlled path and contents:
+        "--add-virtual-file",
     ]
 
     unsafe_git_revision_options =
```

### Fix summary

The patch extends the `unsafe_git_archive_options` denylist to include `--add-file` and `--add-virtual-file`. This prevents these dangerous options from being passed to `git archive` commands, thereby mitigating the arbitrary file read vulnerability.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 62 occurrences**
> Languages: Python, JavaScript, Java, PHP, C#, Go, RUST, Ruby

## GHSA-m65r-rprj-r5rg · russh

2026-08-03 · RUST · **MODERATE** 6.5 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTHZ→RESOURCE`

### Root cause

The `russh` library allowed channel-scoped messages (like `CHANNEL_REQUEST` or `CHANNEL_WINDOW_ADJUST`) to be processed and trigger handler callbacks even if the associated SSH channel had not been fully confirmed or established. This meant an authenticated but unprivileged attacker could interact with channel-specific handlers without a properly opened channel.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially trigger arbitrary channel-scoped callbacks on the server side, leading to unexpected behavior, resource consumption, or potentially bypassing intended access controls if the callbacks performed sensitive operations without proper channel state validation.

### Key diff

```
--- a/russh/src/server/encrypted.rs
+++ b/russh/src/server/encrypted.rs
@@ -984,6 +1089,9 @@ impl Session {
             msg::CHANNEL_CLOSE => {
                 let channel_num = map_err!(ChannelId::decode(r))?;
                 map_err!(ensure_end(r))?;
+                if !self.is_established_channel(channel_num) {
+                    return Ok(());
+                }
                 if let Some(ref mut enc) = self.common.encrypted {
                     // Reply with CHANNEL_CLOSE per RFC 4254 Section 5.3.
                     enc.close(channel_num)?;
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces an `is_established_channel` check before processing channel-scoped messages. This ensures that handler callbacks are only invoked for channels that have been fully confirmed and established, preventing premature or unauthorized interaction with channel handlers.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 84 occurrences**
> Languages: Go, Ruby, JavaScript, Python, PHP, Java, RUST, Erlang, C#

## GHSA-p538-c434-8v24 · GitPython

2026-08-03 · Python · **MODERATE** 5.4 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The `Commit.count` method in GitPython directly passed user-supplied `kwargs` to the underlying `git rev-list` command without proper sanitization. This allowed an attacker to inject arbitrary options, specifically `--output=<file>`, which could redirect the command's output to a specified file, effectively truncating it.

### Impact

An attacker could truncate any file on the system that the GitPython process has write permissions to, leading to data loss or denial of service for other applications relying on those files.

### Key diff

```
--- a/git/objects/commit.py
+++ b/git/objects/commit.py
@@ -284,6 +292,11 @@ def count(self, paths: Union[PathLike, Sequence[PathLike]] = "", **kwargs: Any)
         :return:
             An int defining the number of reachable commits
         """
+        if not allow_unsafe_options:
+            Git.check_unsafe_options(
+                options=Git._option_candidates([], kwargs), unsafe_options=self.unsafe_git_rev_options
+            )
+
         # Yes, it makes a difference whether empty paths are given or not in our case as
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces an `allow_unsafe_options` parameter to the `count` method, which defaults to `False`. When `False`, it explicitly checks for and disallows unsafe options like `--output` from being passed to `git rev-list`, preventing argument injection.

## GHSA-8j4g-w8fx-2239 · hono

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 5.3 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→REGEX`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the application used a regular expression ( /\s*,\s*/ ) to split the 'Access-Control-Request-Headers' header value. An attacker could craft a malicious header value that would cause the regular expression engine to backtrack excessively, leading to a denial of service.

### Impact

An attacker could send a specially crafted HTTP request to the server, causing the application to consume excessive CPU resources and potentially become unresponsive, leading to a denial of service for legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
-	          headers = requestHeaders.split(/\s*,\s*/)
+	          headers = requestHeaders.split(',').map((h) => h.trim())
```

### Fix summary

The patch changes the way the 'Access-Control-Request-Headers' header is parsed. Instead of using a regular expression to split the string, it now uses a simple string split by comma ( .split(',') ) followed by trimming whitespace from each resulting header ( .map((h) => h.trim()) ). This avoids the ReDoS vulnerability.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 11 occurrences**
> Languages: Go, JavaScript, Python, PHP
> → GHSA-fmwg-qcqh-m992
> → GHSA-cjw9-ghj4-fwxf

## GHSA-8xcm-r25x-g524 · undici

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 4.8 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The `undici` HTTP client's retry mechanism did not properly validate the `Content-Length` header for partial responses (206 Partial Content). When retrying a request with a `Range` header, if the server responded with a `Content-Length` that did not match the expected length based on the `Range` header, `undici` would still process the response, leading to a desynchronization between the expected and actual content.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially manipulate the `Content-Length` header in a partial response to cause the downstream application to receive an incomplete or malformed response, leading to data corruption, unexpected application behavior, or potentially other vulnerabilities if the application relies on the integrity of the received data.

### Key diff

```
+function validatePartialResponseContentLength (headers, range, statusCode, retryCount) {
+  const contentLength = headers['content-length']
+  // ...
+  if (!Number.isFinite(length) || length !== expectedLength) {
+    throw new RequestRetryError('Content-Length mismatch', statusCode, {
+      headers,
+      data: { count: retryCount }
+    })
+  }
+}
 // ...
       } else if (statusCode === 206) {
         const contentRange = parseRange(headers['content-range'])
         assert(contentRange, 'content-range header is required for 206 responses')
+        validatePartialResponseContentLength(headers, contentRange, statusCode, this.retryCount)
         const { start, size, end = size ? size - 1 : null } = contentRange
 
         assert(this.start === start, 'content-range mismatch')
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new function, `validatePartialResponseContentLength`, which checks if the `Content-Length` header in a partial response (status code 206) matches the length implied by the `Range` header. If there's a mismatch, a `RequestRetryError` is thrown, preventing the desynchronized response from being processed further.

## GHSA-cq5v-8q36-5273 · aiohttp

2026-08-03 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `BUFFER_OVERFLOW→HEAP`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the error handling path in the C HTTP response parser could incorrectly calculate the `after` buffer offset when processing a malformed chunked response. Specifically, `after.split(b"\r\n", 1)[0]` was applied to a raw memory address, leading to an out-of-bounds read if the error position was near the end of the `data` buffer or if `data` was shorter than expected.

### Impact

An attacker sending a specially crafted malformed HTTP response could trigger an out-of-bounds heap read, potentially leading to information disclosure from adjacent memory regions or a denial of service due to a crash.

### Key diff

```
-                    after = cparser.llhttp_get_error_pos(self._cparser)
-                    before = data[:after - base]
-                    after_b = after.split(b"\r\n", 1)[0]
+                    error_pos = cparser.llhttp_get_error_pos(self._cparser)
+                    error_off = error_pos - base
+                    before = data[:error_off]
+                    after = data[error_off:].split(b"\r\n", 1)[0]
```

### Fix summary

The patch corrects the calculation of the `after` buffer by first determining the `error_off` (offset from the start of `data`) and then slicing `data` correctly to get the `before` and `after` parts. This ensures that `split` is called on a valid slice of the `data` buffer, preventing the out-of-bounds read.

## GHSA-g6cj-pr64-35w5 · cryptography

2026-08-03 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `TIMING_SIDE_CHANNEL→TOKEN_LEAK`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the PKCS#7 EnvelopedData decryption process produced distinguishable error messages and timing differences when an attacker provided malformed encrypted keys. This allowed an attacker to use these differences as an oracle to decrypt sensitive information, specifically the content encryption key.

### Impact

An attacker could exploit this oracle to perform a Bleichenbacher attack, potentially leading to the decryption of the content encryption key. This would allow the attacker to then decrypt the actual encrypted content, compromising the confidentiality of the data.

### Key diff

```
-            let key = match recipient_info.key_encryption_algorithm.oid() {
-                &oid::RSA_OID => {
-                    let padding = types::PKCS1V15.get(py)?.call0()?;
-                    private_key
-                        .call_method1(
-                            pyo3::intern!(py, "decrypt"),
-                            (recipient_info.encrypted_key, &padding),
-                        )?
-                        .extract::<pyo3::pybacked::PyBackedBytes>()?
-                }
-                _ => {
-                    return Err(CryptographyError::from(
-                        exceptions::UnsupportedAlgorithm::new_err((
-                            "Only RSA with PKCS #1 v1.5 padding is currently supported for key decryption.",
-                            exceptions::Reasons::UNSUPPORTED_SERIALIZATION,
-                        )),
-                    ));
-                }
-            };
+            let random_key = crate::backend::rand::get_rand_bytes(py, key_size)?;
+            let key = match private_key.call_method1(
+                pyo3::intern!(py, "decrypt"),
+                (recipient_info.encrypted_key, &padding),
+            ) {
+                Ok(key) => {
+                    let key = key.extract::<pyo3::Bound<'_, pyo3::types::PyBytes>>()?;
+                    if key.as_bytes().len() == key_size {
+                        key
+                    } else {
+                        random_key
+                    }
+                }
+                Err(e) if e.is_instance_of::<pyo3::exceptions::PyValueError>(py) => random_key,
+                Err(e) => return Err(e.into()),
+            };
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the vulnerability by ensuring that all key decryption failures, whether due to invalid padding or incorrect key length, result in the same content decryption error. It achieves this by substituting a randomly generated key of the expected size when key decryption fails, thus preventing an attacker from distinguishing between different types of decryption failures.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 5 occurrences**
> Languages: JavaScript, PHP, Java, Python
> → GHSA-jj6q-rrrf-h66h
> → GHSA-r854-jrxh-36qx
> → GHSA-xmj9-7625-f634
> → GHSA-7r92-3jgr-r65q

## GHSA-jhpw-976m-542j · @angular/common

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an ambiguous cache key generation in Angular's HttpTransferCache. Specifically, the `sortAndConcatParams` function used to sort and concatenate HTTP parameters for the cache key did not correctly handle duplicate parameter names or complex parameter structures, leading to different requests potentially generating the same cache key.

### Impact

An attacker could exploit this by crafting requests that, despite having different parameters, would generate an identical cache key. This could lead to cross-request response reuse, where a user receives a cached response intended for a different request, potentially exposing sensitive data or leading to state poisoning.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/common/http/src/transfer_cache.ts
+++ b/packages/common/http/src/transfer_cache.ts
@@ -368,10 +368,12 @@ function getFilteredHeaders(
 }
 
 function sortAndConcatParams(params: HttpParams | URLSearchParams): string {
-  return [...params.keys()]
-    .sort()
-    .map((k) => `${k}=${params.getAll(k)}`)
-    .join('&');
+  const searchParams = new URLSearchParams(
+    params instanceof URLSearchParams ? params : params.toString(),
+  );
+  searchParams.sort();
+  return searchParams.toString();
 }
 
 function makeCacheKey(
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `sortAndConcatParams` function to use `URLSearchParams` for parameter processing. This ensures that parameters are consistently sorted and serialized, correctly handling duplicate keys and complex structures, thus eliminating ambiguity in cache key generation.

## GHSA-jj27-h5hq-8x99 · @angular/core

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The Angular i18n compiler previously used a simple `startsWith('on')` check to identify potential event handler attributes. This check was too broad and could be bypassed by attributes like `only` or other non-event handler attributes starting with 'on', allowing malicious translations to inject executable JavaScript.

### Impact

An attacker could inject malicious JavaScript into the application through translated content, leading to Cross-Site Scripting (XSS). This could allow them to steal user data, perform actions on behalf of the user, or deface the website.

### Key diff

```
-          if (isTrustedType || name.toLowerCase().startsWith('on')) {
+          if (isTrustedType || isPossibleEventHandler(name)) {
             this._reportError(
               attr,
               `Translating attribute '${name}' is disallowed for security reasons.`,
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new helper function `isPossibleEventHandler` that provides a more robust check for event handler attributes. This function ensures that the attribute name is longer than 2 characters, starts with 'on', and is not the specific word 'only', preventing the translation of such attributes for security reasons.

## GHSA-jwv3-5hgf-82ww · cryptography

2026-08-03 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an inefficient certificate path building algorithm that did not limit the number of signature verification checks. When presented with a chain containing duplicate self-signed intermediate certificates, the algorithm could enter an exponential path-building process, leading to excessive resource consumption.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious certificate chain to trigger a denial of service, causing the application to consume excessive CPU and memory resources, potentially making it unresponsive or crashing it.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/rust/cryptography-x509-verification/src/lib.rs
+++ b/src/rust/cryptography-x509-verification/src/lib.rs
@@ -102,6 +102,15 @@ impl Budget {
             })?;
         Ok(())
     }
+
+    fn signature_check<'chain, B: CryptoOps>(&mut self) -> ValidationResult<'chain, (), B> {
+        self.signature_checks = self.signature_checks.checked_sub(1).ok_or_else(|| {
+            ValidationError::new(ValidationErrorKind::FatalError(
+                "Exceeded maximum signature check limit",
+            ))
+        })?;
+        Ok(())
+    }
 }
 
 struct NameChain<'a, 'chain> {
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `Budget` struct with a `signature_checks` limit, defaulting to 128. This budget is decremented with each signature verification attempt during path building, preventing an attacker from forcing an excessive number of checks. Additionally, the `potential_issuers` function was optimized to prioritize candidates based on Authority Key Identifier (AKI) and Subject Key Identifier (SKI) matches, reducing unnecessary signature checks.

## GHSA-m2h6-j472-rp4c · cryptography

2026-08-03 · Python · **MODERATE** 0.0 · Pattern: `MISSING_VERIFICATION→SIGNATURE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an incorrect implementation of DNS name constraint checking, specifically when evaluating wildcard DNS names within `permittedSubtrees`. The previous logic treated wildcard SANs as if they were exact names, leading to a permissive match where a `permittedSubtrees` constraint of `foo.com` would incorrectly allow a wildcard SAN like `*.bar.foo.com`.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious certificate with a wildcard DNS name that would be improperly validated by the `cryptography` library, allowing them to bypass `permittedSubtrees` restrictions and potentially impersonate legitimate domains or services within a constrained environment.

### Key diff

```
-                match (DNSConstraint::new(constraint.0), DNSPattern::new(name.0)) {
-                    (Some(constraint), Some(name)) => Ok(Applied(constraint.matches(&name))),
+                match (DNSConstraint::new(constraint.0), DNSPattern::new(name.0)) {
+                    (Some(constraint), Some(name)) => Ok(Applied(match kind {
+                        SubtreeKind::Permitted => constraint.permits(&name),
+                        SubtreeKind::Excluded => constraint.excludes(&name),
+                    })),
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `SubtreeKind` enum to differentiate between `permittedSubtrees` and `excludedSubtrees` during evaluation. It refactors the DNS name constraint matching logic to use `permits` and `excludes` methods, ensuring that `permittedSubtrees` correctly requires a wildcard SAN to be fully contained within the constraint, rather than just overlapping.

## GHSA-mfx4-hv73-q22v · aiohttp

2026-08-03 · Python · **MODERATE** 0.0 · Pattern: `RACE_CONDITION→TOCTOU`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from a race condition or improper state management during HTTP protocol upgrades, specifically for WebSockets. The `_upgraded` flag was set too early, before the full request body for the initial HTTP request was processed. This allowed an attacker to send a crafted request that would be interpreted as an upgrade, but then smuggle additional data that would be parsed as a new request after the upgrade, bypassing security controls.

### Impact

An attacker could perform HTTP request smuggling, potentially bypassing security devices like WAFs or reverse proxies, and accessing internal services or injecting malicious requests into the application's request queue.

### Key diff

```
--- a/aiohttp/_http_parser.pyx
+++ b/aiohttp/_http_parser.pyx
@@ -480,10 +482,15 @@ cdef class HttpParser:
                 raise BadHttpMessage("Missing 'Host' header in request.")
             h_upg = headers.get("upgrade", "")
             if (upgrade and h_upg.isascii() and h_upg.lower() in ALLOWED_UPGRADES) or self._cparser.method == cparser.HTTP_CONNECT:
-                self._upgraded = True
+                # https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc9110/#section-7.8-15
+                # Defer the protocol switch until the complete request has been
+                # received.
+                self._pending_upgrade = True
         else:
             if upgrade and self._cparser.status_code == 101:
-                self._upgraded = True
+                # llhttp pauses for a 101 on its own; just mark the pending
+                # switch so feed_data returns the upgraded-protocol tail.
+                self._pending_upgrade = True
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `_pending_upgrade` flag. The `_upgraded` flag is now only set to true after the complete request body has been received and processed, even if an upgrade header is present. This ensures that the protocol switch is deferred until the initial HTTP request is fully parsed, preventing premature upgrade and subsequent request smuggling.

## GHSA-mq44-7p77-q5h7 · aiohttp

2026-08-03 · Python · **MODERATE** 0.0 · Pattern: `INSECURE_DEFAULT→CONFIG`

### Root cause

The aiohttp WebSocket client had a default `compress` parameter set to `True` in its `WebSocketReader` constructor. This meant that the client would attempt to decompress incoming WebSocket frames even if the `permessage-deflate` extension was not successfully negotiated during the WebSocket handshake, leading to potential issues if a malicious server sent compressed frames without negotiation.

### Impact

An attacker (malicious server) could send compressed WebSocket frames to a vulnerable client without proper negotiation. While the immediate impact isn't RCE or data exfiltration, it could lead to unexpected client behavior, resource consumption, or potentially trigger other vulnerabilities in the decompression logic.

### Key diff

```
--- a/aiohttp/client.py
+++ b/aiohttp/client.py
@@ -1370,7 +1370,12 @@ async def _ws_connect(
                 compress=compress,
                 client_notakeover=notakeover,
             )
-            parser = WebSocketReader(reader, max_msg_size, decode_text=decode_text)
+            parser = WebSocketReader(
+                reader,
+                max_msg_size,
+                compress=bool(compress),
+                decode_text=decode_text,
+            )
             cb = None if heartbeat is None else ws_resp._on_data_received
             conn_proto.set_parser(parser, reader, data_received_cb=cb)
             return ws_resp
```

### Fix summary

The patch explicitly passes the `compress` negotiation result from the `_ws_connect` function to the `WebSocketReader` constructor. It also removes the default `compress=True` from the `WebSocketReader`'s `__init__` method, making it mandatory to pass the negotiated compression state.

## GHSA-mwp4-54f8-5fhr · ip-address

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The `ip-address` library's `Address4` class parsed IPv4 octets with leading zeros as decimal values (e.g., `010` as `10`), while standard network resolvers (like WHATWG URL parser, `inet_aton`, `getaddrinfo`) interpret them as octal (e.g., `010` as `8`). This discrepancy allowed an attacker to craft an IP address string that the library would validate as one IP, but the underlying network stack would resolve to a different IP.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass trust boundaries and potentially achieve Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) by tricking an application into connecting to an unintended internal or restricted host, even if the application's validation logic using `ip-address` deemed the target safe.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/v4/constants.ts
+++ b/src/v4/constants.ts
-  /^(25[0-5]|2[0-4][0-9]|[01]?[0-9][0-9]?)\.(25[0-5]|2[0-4][0-9]|[01]?[0-9][0-9]?)\.(25[0-5]|2[0-4][0-9]|[01]?[0-9][0-9]?)\.(25[0-5]|2[0-4][0-9]|[01]?[0-9][0-9]?)$/g;
+  /^(25[0-5]|2[0-4][0-9]|1[0-9][0-9]|[1-9]?[0-9])\.(25[0-5]|2[0-4][0-9]|1[0-9][0-9]|[1-9]?[0-9])\.(25[0-5]|2[0-4][0-9]|1[0-9][0-9]|[1-9]?[0-9])\.(25[0-5]|2[0-4][0-9]|1[0-9][0-9]|[1-9]?[0-9])$/g;
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `Address4` and `Address6` classes to explicitly reject IPv4 addresses containing leading zeros in any octet. It also updates the `RE_ADDRESS` regular expression to prevent matching octets with leading zeros, ensuring that the library's parsing behavior aligns with standard network stack interpretations.

## GHSA-vpx6-8pjr-4g3v · @angular/platform-server

2026-08-03 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because Angular's Server-Side Rendering (SSR) did not properly escape raw content within certain HTML elements (like <noscript>, <iframe>) when they were re-parsed by downstream post-processing tools. While these elements are typically inert for browser parsing, the lack of escaping for their inner text allowed an attacker to inject malicious HTML/JavaScript if the content was later re-interpreted as active HTML.

### Impact

An attacker could inject arbitrary HTML and JavaScript into the rendered page, leading to Cross-Site Scripting (XSS). This could allow them to steal user session cookies, deface the website, or redirect users to malicious sites.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/NodeUtils.js
+++ b/lib/NodeUtils.js
@@ -215,7 +266,9 @@ function serializeOne(kid, parent) {
         parenttag = '';
 
       if (hasRawContent[parenttag]) {
-        s += kid.data;
+        // Preserve actual child element markup in fallback elements such as
+        // <noscript>, but do not emit text-node payloads as raw HTML.
+        s += hasRawContentFallback[parenttag] ? escapeFallbackRawText(kid.data, parent.localName) : kid.data;
       } else {
         s += escape(kid.data);
       }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `hasRawContentFallback` list for elements that require special handling. It adds a new `escapeFallbackRawText` function to properly escape content within these fallback raw-content elements, specifically handling HTML comments and ensuring that closing tags are correctly escaped to prevent XSS. The `serializeOne` function was updated to use this new escaping logic for fallback elements.
